It cal also\nbe computed on-the-fly (ST norm from LUT) with\n∥x′∥2 = 2\nM\nX\nm=1\nm−1\nX\nℓ=1\n⟨Tm[cm], Tℓ[cℓ]⟩+\nM\nX\nm=1\n∥Tm[cm]∥2\n(16)\nWhere the norms and dot products are stored in the\nsame lookup tables as the one used for beam search.\nTherefore, it is a tradeoff between memory overhead\nto store codes and search time overhead.\nFigure 3 shows the tradeoff between encoding time\nand MSE. For a given code size, it is more accurate\nto use a smaller number of sub-quantizers M and a\nhigher K. GPU encoding for LSQ does not help sys-\ntematically.\nThe LUT-based encoding of RQ is in-\nteresing for RQ/PRQ quantization when the beam\nsize is larger. For the 64-byte regime, we observe that\nLSQ is not competitive with RQ. PLSQ and PRQ pro-\ngressively become more competitive for larger mem-\nory budgets. They are also faster, since they operate\non smaller vectors.\n4.4\nVector compression benchmark\nFigure 4 shows the tradeoff between code size and\naccuracy for many variants of the codecs.\nAddi-\ntive quantizers are the best options for small code\nsizes.\nFor larger code sizes it is beneficial to inde-\npendently encode several sub-vectors with product-\nadditive quantizers. LSQ is more accurate than RQ for\nsmall codes, but does not scale well to longer codes.\nNote that product quantizers are a bits less accurate\nthan the additive quantizers but given how efficient\nthey are this remains an attractive option. The scalar\nquantizers perform well for very long codes and are\neven faster. The 2-level PQ options are what an IVFPQ\nindex uses as encoding: a first-level coarse quantizer\n9\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\nand a second level refinement of the residual (more\nabout this in Section 5.1).\n4.5\nBinary indexes\nBinary quantization with symmetric distance com-\nputations is a pattern that has been commonly\nused [Wang et al., 2015, Cao et al., 2017]. In this setup,\ndistances are computed in the compressed domain as\nHamming distances. Equation (11) reduces to:\nn = argmin\nn=1..N\n∥C(q) −Cn∥\n(17)\nwhere C(q), Cn ∈{0, 1}d. Although binary quantizers\nare crude approximations for continuous domain dis-\ntances, the Hamming distances are integers in {0..d},\nthat are fast to compute, do not require any specific\ncontext, and are easy to calibrate in practice.\nThe Faiss IndexBinary indexes support addition\nand search directly from binary vectors. They offer\na compact representation and leverage optimized in-\nstructions for distance computations.\nThe simplest IndexBinaryFlat index performs ex-\nhaustive search. Three options are offered for non-\nexhaustive search:\n• IndexBinaryIVF is a binary counterpart for the\ninverted-list IndexIVF index described in 5.1.\n• IndexBinaryHNSW is a binary counterpart for the\nhierarchical graph-based IndexHNSW index de-\nscribed in 5.2.\n• IndexBinaryHash uses prefix vectors as hashes\nto cluster the database (rather than spheroids as\nwith inverted lists), and searches only the clusters\nwith closests prefixes.\nFinally, a convenience IndexBinaryFromFloat in-\ndex is provided that simply wraps an arbitrary index\nand offers a binary vector interface for its operations.\n5\nNon-exhaustive search\nNon-exhaustive search is the cornerstone of fast\nsearch implementations for medium-sized datasets.\nIn that case, the aim of the indexing method is to\nquickly focus on a subset of database vectors that are\nmost likely to contain the search results.\nAn early method to do this is Locality Sensitive\nHashing (LSH). It amounts to projecting the vectors\non a random direction [Datar et al., 2004].\nThe off-\nsets on that direction are then discretized into buckets\nwhere the database vectors are stored. At search time,\nthe buckets nearest to the query vector’s projection are\nvisited. In practice, several projection directions are\nneeded to make it accurate, at the cost of search time.\nA fundamental drawback of this method is that it is\nnot data-adaptive, although some improvements are\npossible [Paulev´e et al., 2010].\nAn alternative way of pruning the search space is\nto use tree-based indexing. In that case, the dataset is\nstored in the leaves of a tree [Muja and Lowe, 2014].\nWhen querying a vector, the search starts at the root\nnode. At each internal node, the search descends into\none of the child nodes depending on a decision rule.\nThe decision rule depends on how the tree was built:\nfor a KD-tree it is the position w.r.t. a hyperplane, for a\nhierarchical k-means, it is the proximity to a centroid.\nBoth in the case of LSH and tree-based methods,\nthe hope is to extend classical database search struc-\ntures to vector search, because they have a favorable\ncomplexity (constant or logarithmic in N). However,\nit turns out that these methods do not scale well for\ndimensions above 10.\nFaiss implements two non-exhaustive search ap-\nproaches that operate at different memory vs. speed\ntradeoffs: inverted file and graph-based.\n5.1\nInverted files\nIVF indexing is a technique that clusters the database\nvectors at indexing time. This clustering uses a vec-\ntor quantizer (the coarse quantizer) that outputs KIVF\ndistinct indices (Faiss’s nlist parameter). The coarse\nquantizer’s KIVF reproduction values are called cen-\ntroids.\nThe vectors of each cluster (possibly com-\npressed) are stored contiguously into inverted lists,\nforming an inverted file (IVF). At search time, only a\nsubset of PIVF clusters are visited (a.k.a. nprobe). The\nsubset is formed by searching the PIVF nearest cen-\ntroids, as in Equation (2).\nSetting the number of lists.\nThe KIVF parameter is\ncentral. In the simplest case, when PIVF is fixed, the\ncoarse quantizer is exhaustive, the inverted lists con-\ntain uncompressed vectors, and the inverted lists are\nall the same size, then the number of distance compu-\ntations is\nNdistances = KIVF + PIVF × N/KIVF\n(18)\nwhich reaches a minimum when KIVF = √PIVFN.\nThis yields the usual recommendation to set PIVF pro-\nportional to\n√\nN.\nIn practice, this is just a rough approximation be-\ncause (1) the PIVF has to increase with the number\nof lists in order to keep a fixed accuracy (2) often the\ncoarse quantizer is not exhaustive itself, so the quanti-\nzation uses fewer than KIVF distance computations,\nfor example it is common to use a non-exhaustive\nHNSW index to perform the coarse quantization.\nFigure 5 shows the optimal settings of KIVF for\nvarious database sizes.\nFor a small KIVF = 4096,\nthe coarse quantization runtime is negligible and the\nsearch time increases linearly with the database size.\nFor larger datasets it is beneficial to increase the KIVF.\nAs in equation (18), the ratio KIVF/\n√\nN is roughly 15\nto 20. Note that this ratio depends on the data dis-\ntribution and the target accuracy. Interestingly, in a\nregime where KIVF is larger than the optimal setting\nfor N (e.g. KIVF = 218 and N =5M), the PIVF needed\nto reach the target accuracy decreases with the dataset\n10\n\n\n=== PDF page 11 ===\n1M\n2M\n5M\n10M\n20M\n50M\ndatabase size\n0.2\n0.5\n1\n2\n5\nsearch time (s for 10k queries, 64 threads)\n 4.1\n 5.8\n 14.7\n 14.7\n 20.7\n 20.7\n 14.7\n 29.3\n 18.5\n 37.1\ntarget recall 0.9\nKIVF = 4096\nKIVF = 8192\nKIVF = 16384\nKIVF = 32768\nKIVF = 65536\nKIVF = 131072\nKIVF = 262144\nscaling model\nFigure 5: Search time as a function of the database size N for Bi-\ngANN1M with different KIVF settings. The PIVF is set so that the\n1-recall@1 is 90%. The full lines indicate that the coarse quantizer is\nexact, the dashed lines rely on a HNSW coarse quantizer. For some\nsetting we indicate the ratio KIVF/\n√\nN\nsize, and so does the search time.\nThis is because\nwhen KIVF is fixed and N increases, for a given query\nvector, the nearest database vector is either the same\nor a new one that is closer, so it is more likely to be\nfound in a quantization cluster nearer to the query.\nWith a faster non-exhaustive coarse quantizer (e.g.\nHNSW) it is even more useful to increase KIVF for\nlarger databases, as the coarse quantization becomes\nrelatively cheap. At the limit, when KIVF = N, then\nall the work is done by the coarse quantizer. However,\nin that case the limiting factor becomes the memory\noverhead of the coarse quantizer.\nBy fitting a model of the form t = t0N α to the tim-\nings of the fastest index in Figure 5, we can derive a\nscaling rule for the IVF indexes:\ntarget recall@1\n0.5\n0.75\n0.9\n0.99\npower α\n0.29\n0.30\n0.34\n0.45\nThus, with this model, the search time increases faster\nfor higher accuracy targets, but α < 0.5, so the run-\ntime dependence on the database size is below\n√\nN.\nEncoding residuals.\nIn general, it is more accurate\nto compress the residuals of the database vectors w.r.t.\nthe centroids [J´egou et al., 2010, Equation (28)]. This is\nbecause the norm of the residuals is lower than that of\nthe original vectors, or because residual encoding is\na way to take into account a-priori information from\nthe coarse quantizer. In Faiss, this is controlled via\nthe IndexIVF.by residual flag, which is set to true\nby default.\nFigure 6 shows that encoding residuals is beneficial\nfor shorter codes. For larger codes, the contribution\nof the residual is less important. Indeed, as the orig-\ninal data is 96-dimensional, it can be compressed to\n64 bytes relatively accurately. Note that using higher\nKIVF also improves the accuracy of the quantizer with\nresidual encoding.\nFrom a pure encoding point of\nview, the additional bits of information brought by the\nPQ4\nPQ6\nPQ8\nPQ8x10\nPQ12\nPQ12x10\nPQ16x10\nPQ24\nPQ24x10\nPQ32x10\nPQ48\nPQ48x10\nPQ48x12\n4\n8\n16\n32\n64\n128\ncode size (bytes)\n0.0\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\n0.8\nrecall @ 1 for ndis < 3000\nKIVF = 1024, direct encoding\nKIVF = 1024, encode residuals\nKIVF = 16384, direct encoding\nKIVF = 16384, encode residuals\nFigure 6: Comparing IVF indexes with and without residual encod-\ning for KIVF ∈{210, 214} on the Deep1M dataset (d=96 dimen-\nsions), with different product quantization settings. We measure\nthe recall that can be achieved within 3000 distance comparisons.\ncoarse quantizer (log2(KIVF) = 10 or 14) improve the\naccuracy more when used in this residual encoding\nthan if they would added to increase the size of a PQ.\nSpherical clustering for inner product search\nEf-\nficient indexing for maximum inner product search\n(MIPS) faces multiple issues: the distribution of query\nvectors is often different from the database vector\ndistribution, most notably in recommendation sys-\ntems [Paterek, 2007]; the MIPS datasets are diverse,\nan algorithm that obtains a good performance on\nsome dataset will perform badly on another.\nBe-\nsides, [Morozov and Babenko, 2018] show that using\nthe preprocessing formulas in Section 3 is a subopti-\nmal way of indexing for MIPS.\nSeveral specialized clustering and indexing meth-\nods\nwere\ndeveloped\nfor\nMIPS\n[Guo et al., 2020,\nMorozov and Babenko, 2018].\nInstead, Faiss imple-\nments a simple modification of k-means clustering,\nspherical k-means [Dhillon and Modha, 2001], that\nnormalizes the k-means centroids at each iteration.\nThe MIPS issues appear strongly when vectors are\nof very different norms (when the database vectors are\nnormalized, MIPS becomes equivalent to L2 search).\nFor IVF, this manifests itself with a high imbalance\nfactor, which is the relative variance of inverted list\nsizes [Tavenard et al., 2011]. At search time, if the in-\nverted lists are perfectly balanced (i.e. all have the\nsame length), this factor is 1. If they are unbalanced,\nthe number of distances computed for a given PIVF\nincreases with the imbalance factor.\nThe imbalance is mainly due to high-norm cen-\ntroids that attract the database vectors in their clus-\nters. To avoid this, we can normalize the centroids at\neach iteration.\nFigure 7 shows that for the contriever MIPS dataset,\nthe imbalance factor is quite high. It can be reduced\nby using IP assignment instead of L2, and even more\nby L2-normalizing the centroids at each k-means iter-\n11\n\n\n=== PDF page 12 ===\n0.4\n0.5\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n0.9\n1.0\n1-recall@1\n102\n103\n104\nQPS\nKIVF = 1024, default: imbalance=2.23\nKIVF = 16384, default: imbalance=3.13\nKIVF = 1024, IP assignment: imbalance=1.16\nKIVF = 16384, IP assignment: imbalance=2.17\nKIVF = 1024, spherical: imbalance=1.12\nKIVF = 16384, spherical: imbalance=1.39\nFigure 7: Precision vs. speed tradeoff for the MIPS contriever1M\ndataset. The parameters are whether the coarse quantizer assigne-\nment is done using L2 distance (default) or MIPS and whether the k-\nmeans clustering is the regular one or with normalization at each it-\neration (spherical, IP and L2 assignment are equivalent in that case).\nThe imbalance factors are indicated for each setting.\nation (spherical is set to true).\nBig batch search\nA common use case for ANNS is\nsearch with very large query batches. This appears\nfor applications such as large-scale data deduplica-\ntion. In this case, rather than loading an entire index\nin memory and processing queries one small batch\nat a time, it can be more memory-efficient to load\nonly the quantizer, quantize the queries, and then it-\nerate over the index by loading it one chunk at a\ntime. Big-batch search is implemented in the module\ncontrib.big batch search.\n5.2\nGraph based\nGraph-based indexing consists in building a directed\ngraph whose nodes are the vectors to index. At search\ntime, the graph is explored by following the edges to-\nwards the nodes that are closest to the query vector.\nIn practice, the search is not greedy but maintains a\npriority queue with the most promising edges to ex-\nplore. Thus, the tradeoff at search time is given by the\nnumber of exploration steps: higher is more accurate\nbut slower.\nA graph-based algorithm is a general framework\nin fact.\nTree-based search or IVF can be seen as\nspecial cases of graphs.\nGraphs can be seen as a\nway to precompute neighbors for the database vec-\ntors, then match the query to one of the vertices\nand follow the neighbors from there.\nHowever,\nthey can also be built to handle out-of-distribution\nqueries [Jaiswal et al., 2022].\nGiven the search algorithm, taking a pure k-nearest\nneighbor graph is not optimal because the greedy\nsearch is prone to finding local minima. Therefore, the\ngraph building heuristic consists in balancing edges\nto nearest neighbors and edges that reach more dis-\ntant nodes. Most graph methods fix the number of\noutgoing edges per node, which adjusts the tradeoff\n0.70\n0.75\n0.80\n0.85\n0.90\n0.95\n1.00\n1-recall@1\n104\n105\n106\nQueries per second\n64 \n128 \n256 \n512 \n1024 \n64 \n128 \n256 \n512 \n1024 \n2048 \n8 edges\n16 edges\n32 edges\n64 edges\nFigure 8: Comparison of graph-based indexing methods HNSW\n(full lines) and NSG (dashes) to index Deep1M. We sweep the trade-\noffs between speed and accuracy by varying the number of graph\ntraversal steps (indicated for some of the curves).\nbetween search speed and memory usage. The mem-\nory usage per vector breaks down into (1) the possibly\ncompressed vector and (2) the outgoing edges for that\nvector [Douze et al., 2018].\nFaiss implements two graph-based algorithms:\nHNSW and NSG, in the IndexHNSW and IndexNSG\nclasses, respectively.\nHNSW\nThe hierarchical navigable small world\ngraph [Malkov and Yashunin, 2018] is an elegant\nsearch structure where some vertices (nodes), selected\nrandomly, are promoted to be hubs that are explored\nfirst. One of the attractive properties of HNSW is that\nit is built incrementally. As a consequence, vectors can\nbe added to it on-the-fly.\nNSG\nThe\nNavigating\nSpreading-out\nGraph [Fu et al., 2017] is built from a k-nearest\nneighbor graph that must be provided on input. At\nbuilding time, some short-range edges are replaced\nwith longer-range edges. The input k-nn graph can\nbe built with a brute force algorithm or with a special-\nized method such as NN-descent [Dong et al., 2011]\n(NNDescent). Unlike HNSW, NSG does not rely on\nmulti-layer graph structures, but uses long connec-\ntions to achieve fast navigation.\nIn addition, NSG\nstarts from a fixed center point when searching.\nDiscussion\nFigure 8 compares the speed vs.\nac-\ncuracy for the NSG and HNSW indexes. The main\nbuild-time hyper-parameter of the two methods is the\nnumber of edges per node, so we tested several set-\ntings (for HNSW this is the number of edges on the\nbase level of the hierachical graph). The main search-\ntime parameter is the number of graph traversal steps\nduring search (parameter efSearch for HNSW and\nsearch L for NSG), which we vary to plot each curve.\nIncreasing the number of edges improves the results\nonly to some extent: beyond 64 edges it degrades.\n12\n\n\n=== PDF page 13 ===\nNSG obtains better tradeoffs in general, at the cost of\na longer build time. Building the input k-NN graph\nwith NN-descent for 1M vectors takes 37 s, and about\nthe same time with brute force search on a GPU (but\nin that case the k-NN graph is exact). The NSG graph\nis frozen after the first batch of vectors is added, there\nis no easy way to add more vectors afterwards.\nIVF vs. graph-based.\nAn IVF index can be seen as\na special case of graph-based index, especially if a\nsmall graph-based index is used as coarse quantizer.\nIn Faiss, graph-based indices are a good option for in-\ndexes where there is no constraint on memory usage,\ntypically for indexes below 1M vectors. Beyond 10M\nvectors, the construction time typically becomes the\nlimiting factor. For larger indexes, where compression\nis required to even fit the database vectors in memory,\nIVF indexes are the only option. However, as shown\nin Section 5.1, the optimal number of centroids is so\nlarge that a graph-based index should be used to per-\nform the coarse quantization, and much of the search\ntime is spent in the coarse quantization.\n6\nDatabase operations\nIn all the experiments above, the indexes are built\nin one go with all the vectors, while search oper-\nations are performed with one batch containing all\nquery vectors. In real settings, the index evolves over\ntime, vectors may be dynamically added or removed,\nsearches may have to take into account metadata, etc.\nIn this section we show how Faiss supports these\noperations to some extent, mainly on IVF indexes.\nWhen more fine-grained control is required, there are\nspecific APIs to interface with external storage (Sec-\ntion 7.4).\n6.1\nIdentifier-based operations\nFaiss indexes support two types of identifiers: sequen-\ntial ids are based on the order of additions in the in-\ndex. On the other hand, the user can provide arbi-\ntrary 63-bit integer ids along with each vector. The\ncorresponding addition methods for the index are add\nand add with ids. In addition, Faiss supports remov-\ning vectors (remove ids) and updating vector them\n(update vectors) by passing the corresponding ids.\nUnlike e.g. Usearch [Vardanian, 2022], Faiss does\nnot store arbitrary metadata with the vectors, only 63-\nbit integer ids can be used (the sign bit is reserved for\ninvalid results).\nFlat indexes.\nSequential indexes (IndexFlatCodes)\nstore vectors as a flat array. They support only se-\nquential ids. When arbitrary ids are needed, the index\ncan be embedded in a IndexIDMap, that translates se-\nquence numbers to arbitrary ids using a int64 array.\nThis enables add with ids and returns the arbitrary\nids at search time. To also perform id-based opera-\ntions, a more powerful wrapper class, IndexIDMap2,\nuses a hash table that maps arbitrary ids back to the\nsequential ids.\nSince\ngraph\nindexes\nrely\non\nan\nembedded\nIndexFlatCodes to store the actual vectors, they\nshould also be wrapped with the mapping classes.\nNote however that HNSW does not support sup-\npression and mutation, and that NSG does not even\nsupport adding vectors incrementally.\nSupporting\nthis requires heuristics to re-build the graph when\nit is mutated, which are implemented in HNSWlib\nand [Singh et al., 2021] but could be suboptimal\nindexing-wise.\nIVF indexes.\nThe IVF indexing structure does sup-\nports user-provided ids natively at addition and\nsearch time. However, id-based access may require\na sequential scan, since the entries are stored in an ar-\nbitrary order in the inverted lists. Therefore, the IVF\nindex can optionally maintain a DirectMap, that maps\nuser-visible ids to the inverted list and the offset they\nare stored in. The map can be an array, which is ap-\npropriate for sequential ids, or a hash table, for ar-\nbitrary 63-bit ids. Obviously, the direct map incurs\na memory overhead and an add-time computation\noverhead, therefore it is disabled by default. When\nthe direct map is enabled, lookup, removal and up-\ndate are supported.\n6.2\nFiltered search\nVector filtering consists in returning only database\nvectors based on some search-time criterion, other\nvectors are ignored. Faiss has basic support for vector\nfiltering: the user can provide a predicate (IDSelector\ncallback), and if the predicate returns false on the vec-\ntor id, the vector is ignored.\nTherefore, if metadata is needed to filter the vec-\ntors, the callback function needs to do an indirection\nto the metadata table, which is inefficient. Another\napproach is to exploit the unused bits of the identi-\nfier. If n documents are indexed with sequential ids,\n63 −⌈log2(N)⌉bits are unused.\nThis is sufficient to store enumerated types (e.g. .\ncountry codes, music genres, license types, etc.), dates\n(as days since some origin), version numbers, etc.\nHowever, it is insufficient for more complex metadata.\nIn the example use case below, we use the available\nbits to implement a more complex filtering method.\nFiltering with bag-of-word vectors.\n4\nEach query\nand database vector has is associated with a few terms\nfrom a fixed vocabulary of size v (for the queries there\nare only 1 or 2 words). The filtering consists in con-\nsidering only the database vectors that include all the\nquery terms. This metadata is given as a sparse matrix\nMmeta ∈{0, 1}N×v.\nThe basic implementation of the filter uses query\nvector q and the associated words w1, w2 ∈{1...v}.\n4This example is from the the BigANN’23 challenge, https://\nbig-ann-benchmarks.com/neurips23.html.\n13\n\n\n=== PDF page 14 ===\nBefore computing a distance to a vector with id i, it\nfetches row i of Mmeta to verify that w1 and w2 are\nin it. This predicate is relatively slow because (1) it\nrequires to access Mmeta, which causes cache misses\nand (2) it performs an iterative binary search. Since\nthe callback is called in the tightest inner loop of the\nsearch function, and since the IVF search tends to per-\nform many vector comparisons, this has non negligi-\nble performance impact.\nTo speed up this test, we can use a nifty piece of\nbit manipulation. In this example, N = 107, so we\nuse only ⌈log2 N⌉= 24 bits of the ids, leaving 63 −\n24 = 39 bits that are always 0. We associate to each\nword j a 39-bit signature S[j], and the to each set of\nwords the binary “or” of these signatures. The query\nis represented by sq = S[w1] ∨S[w2]. Database entry\ni with words Wi is represented by si = ∨w∈WiS[w].\nThen we have the following implication: if {w1, w2} ⊂\nWi then all 1 bits of sq are also set to 1 in si:\n{w1, w2} ⊂Wi ⇒¬si ∧sq = 0\n(19)\nwhich is equivalent to:\n¬si ∧sq ̸= 0 ⇒{w1, w2} ̸⊂Wi\n(20)\nThis binary test is very cheap to perform: a few ma-\nchine instructions on data that is already in machine\nregisters. It can thus be used as a pre-filter to apply\nthe full membership test on candidates.\nThe remaining degree of freedom is how to choose\nthe binary signatures, because this rule is always\nvalid, but its filtering ability depends on the choice of\nthe signatures S. We experimented with iid Bernouilli\nbits with varying p.\np= probability of 1\n0.05\n0.1\n0.2\n0.5\nFilter hit rate\n75.4%\n82.1%\n76.4%\n42.0%\ni.e. the best setting avoids to do the full check more\nthan 4/5th of the times.\nPre- or post-filtering.\nThere are two possible ap-\nproaches to filtered search: post-filtering, which is de-\nscribed above, and pre-filtering, where only vectors\nwith appropriate metadata are considered in vector\nsearch. The pre-filtering generates a subset of vectors\nto compare with.\nTherefore, the decision to use pre- or post-filtering\ndepends on whether the subset of vectors is large or\nnot. This can be estimated prior to the search depend-\ning on the filtering ability of the query metadata.\nIn the bag-of-words example, pre-filtering can ex-\nploit a term-based inverted file, which is the Mmeta\nrepresented in compressed sparse column format. The\nproduct of the frequencies of w1 and w2 is an estimate\nof what fraction of vectors pre-filtering will perform.\n7\nFaiss engineering\nFaiss started in a research environment. As a conse-\nquence, it grew organically, one index at a time, as\nIndex implementations\nIndexIVFPQ, IndexFlat, etc.\nGPU index implementations\nGpuIndexIVFPQ, …\nCUDA library\nBLAS library \n(MKL, openblas)\nUtilities and primitives\nHeap, ProductQuantizer, Clustering …\nSWIG wrapper (Python)\nswigfaiss.swig → swigfaiss.py\nAdaptor layer \n__init__.py, class_enhancement.py\nC wrapper \nPython contrib library\n(contrib.datasets, etc.)\nRust / C#  code\nC++ code \ndemos /  tests \nPython code \ntests / benchs\nNumpy library\nRust wrapper \nC# wrapper,...\nFaiss\nC++/cuda\n cppcontrib library\n(SADecodeKernels...)\nFigure 9: Architecture of the Faiss library. Arrows indicate depen-\ndencies. At the bottom are the library’s dependencies, at the top are\nexample software that depends on Faiss, most notably its extensive\ntest suite.\nindexing research was making progress. In the fol-\nlowing, we briefly mention the guiding principles that\nkept the library coherent, how optimization is per-\nformed and an example of how Faiss internals are ex-\nposed so that it can be embedded in a vector database.\n7.1\nCode structure\nThe core of Faiss is implemented in C++. The guiding\nprinciples are (1) the code should be as open as pos-\nsible, so that users can access all the implementation\ndetails of the indexes; (2) Faiss should be easy to em-\nbed from external libraries; (3) the core library focuses\non vector search only.\nTherefore, all fields of the classes are public (C++\nstruct). Faiss is a late adopter for C++ standards, so\nthat it can be used with relatively old compilers (cur-\nrently C++17).\nFaiss’s basic data types are concrete (not templates):\nvectors are always represented as 32-bit floats that are\nportable and provide a good tradeoff between size\nand accuracy. Similarly, all vector ids are represented\nwith 64-bit integers. This is often larger than neces-\nsary for sequential numbering but is widely used for\ndatabase identifiers.\n7.2\nHigh-level interface\nFigure 9 shows the structure of the library. The C++\ncore library and the GPU add-on have as few depen-\ndencies as possible: only a BLAS implementation and\nCUDA itself.\nIn order to facilitate experimentation, the whole\nlibrary is wrapped for scripting languages such as\nPython with numpy.\nTo this end, SWIG5 exhaus-\ntively generates wrappers for all C++ classes, methods\nand variables. The associated Python layer also con-\ntains benchmarking code, dataset definitions, driver\ncode. More and more functionality is embedded in\n5https://www.swig.org/\n14\n\n\n=== PDF page 15 ===\nthe contrib package of Faiss. Faiss also provides a\npure C API, which is useful for producing bindings\nfor programming languages such as Rust or Java.\nThe Index is presented to the end user as a mono-\nlithic object, even when it embeds other indexes\nas quantizers, refinement indexes or sharded sub-\nindexes.\nTherefore, an index can be duplicated\nwith clone index and serialized into as a single byte\nstream using a single function, write index. It also\ncontains the necessary headers so that it can be read\nby a generic function, read index.\nIndex objects can be instantiated explicitly in C++\nor Python, but it is more common to build them\nwith the index factory function.\nThis function\ntakes a string that describes the index structure\nand its main parameters.\nFor example, the string\nPCA160,IVF20000_HNSW,PQ20x10,RFlat instan-\ntiates an IVF index with KIVF = 20000, where the\ncoarse quantizer is a HNSW index; then the vectors\nare represented with a PQ20x10 product quantizer.\nThe data is preprocessed with a PCA to 160 dimen-\nsions, and the search results are re-ranked with a re-\nfinement index that performs exact distance computa-\ntions. All the index parameters are set to reasonable\ndefaults, e.g. the PQ encodes the residual of the vec-\ntors w.r.t. the coarse quantization centroids.\nFaiss provides dedicated standalone vector codec\nfunctions sa encode, sa decode and sa code size as\na part of Index API.\n7.3\nOptimization\nApproach to optimization.\nFaiss aims at being fea-\nture complete first. A non-optimal version of all in-\ndexes is implemented first. Code is optimized only\nwhen it appears that runtime is important for a cer-\ntain index. The non-optimized setting is used to con-\ntrol the correctness of the optimized version.\nOften, only a subset of data sizes are optimized. For\nexample, for PQ indexes, only K = 28 and K = 24\nand d/M ∈{2, 4, 8, 16, 20} are fully optimized. For\nIndexLSH search, only code sizes 4, 8, 16, 20 are opti-\nmized. Fixing these sizes allows to write “kernels”, se-\nquences of instructions without explicit loops or tests,\nthat aim to maximize arithmetic throughput.\nWhen generic scalar CPU optimizations are ex-\nhausted, Faiss also optimizes specifically for some\nhardware platforms.\nCPU vectorization.\nModern CPUs support Single\nInstruction, Multiple Data (SIMD) operations, specif-\nically AVX/AVX2 for x86 and NEON for ARM. Faiss\nexploits those at three levels.\nWhen operations are simple enough (e.g. element-\nwise vector sum), the code is written in a way that the\ncompiler can vectorize the code by itself, which often\nboils down to adding restrict keywords to signal\nthat arrays are not overlapping.\nThe second level leverages SIMD variables and in-\nstructions through C++ compiler extensions.\nFaiss\nincludes simdlib, a collection of classes intended as\na layer above the AVX and NEON instruction sets.\nHowever, much of the SIMD is done specifically for\none instruction set – most often AVX – because it is\nmore efficient.\nThe third level of optimization is to adapt the\ndata layout and algorithms in order to speed up\ntheir SIMD implementation.\nThe 4-bit product\nand additive quantizer implementations are imple-\nmented in this way, inspired by the SCANN li-\nbrary [Guo et al., 2020]: the layout of the PQ codes\nfor several consecutive vectors is interleaved in mem-\nory so that a vector permutation can be used to\nperform the LUT lookups of Equation (14) in par-\nallel.\nThis is implemented in the FastScan vari-\nants of PQ and AQ indexes (IndexPQFastScan,\nIndexIVFResidualQuantizerFastScan, etc.).\nGPU Faiss.\nPorting Faiss to the GPU is an involved\nundertaking due to substantial architectural specifici-\nties. The implementation of GPU Faiss is detailed in\n[Johnson et al., 2019], we summarize the GPU imple-\nmentation challenges therein.\nModern multi-core CPUs are highly latency opti-\nmized:\nthey employ an extensive cache hierarchy,\nbranch prediction, speculative execution and out-of-\norder code execution to improve serial program exe-\ncution. In contrast, GPUs have a limited cache hier-\narchy and omit many of these latency optimizations.\nThey instead possess a larger number of concurrent\nthreads of execution (Nvidia’s A100 GPU allows for\nup to 6,912 warps, each roughly equivalent to a 32-\nwide vector SIMD CPU thread of execution), a large\nnumber of floating-point and integer arithmetic func-\ntional units (A100 has up to 19.5 teraflops per sec-\nond of fp32 fused-multiply add throughput), and a\nmassive register set to allow for a high number of\nlong latency pending instructions in flight (A100 has\n27 MiB of register memory).\nThey are thus largely\nthroughput-optimized machines.\nThe algorithmic techniques used in vector search\ncan be grouped into three broad categories:\ndis-\ntance computation of floating-point or binary vectors\n(which may have been produced via dequantization\nfrom a compressed form), table lookups (as seen in\nPQ distance computations) or scanning (as seen when\ntraversing IVF lists), and irregular, sequential compu-\ntations such as linked-list traversal (as used in graph-\nbased indices) or ranking the k closest vectors.\nDistance computation is easy on GPUs and read-\nily exceeds CPU performance, as GPUs are optimized\nfor matrix-matrix multiplication such as that seen in\nIndexFlat or IVFFlat. Table lookups and list scan-\nning can also be made performant on GPUs, as it is\npossible to stage small tables (as seen in product quan-\ntization) in shared memory (roughly a user-controlled\nL1 cache) or register memory and perform lookups in\nparallel across all warps.\nSequential table scanning in IVF indices requires\nloading data from main (global) memory. While laten-\n15\n\n\n=== PDF page 16 ===\ncies to access main memory are high, for table scan-\nning we know in advance what data we wish to ac-\ncess, so the data movement from main memory into\nregisters can be pipelined or use double buffering, so\nwe can achieve close to peak possible performance.\nSelecting the k closest vectors to a query vector by\nranking distances on the CPU is best implemented\nwith a min- or max-heap. On the GPU, the sequential\noperations involved in heap operations would simi-\nlarly force the GPU into a latency-bound regime. This\nis the largest challenge for GPU implementation of\nvector search, as the time needed for the heap im-\nplementation an order of magnitude greater than all\nother arithmetic. To handle this, we developed an effi-\ncient GPU k-selection algorithm [Johnson et al., 2019]\nthat allows for ranking candidate vectors in a single\npass, operating at a substantial fraction of peak pos-\nsible performance per memory bandwidth limits. It\nrelies upon heavy usage of the high-speed, large reg-\nister memory on GPUs, and small-set bitonic sorting\nvia warp shuffles with buffering techniques.\nIrregular computations such as walking graph\nstructures for graph-based indices like HNSW tend\nto remain in the latency-bound (due to the sequen-\ntial traversal) rather than arithmetic throughput or\nmemory bandwidth-bound regimes. Here, GPUs are\nat a disadvantage as compared to CPUs, and emerg-\ning techniques such as CAGRA [Ootomo et al., 2023]\nare required to parallelize otherwise sequential oper-\nations with graph traversal.\nGPU Faiss implements brute-force GpuIndexFlat\nas\nwell\nas\nthe\nIVF\nindices\nGpuIndexIVFFlat,\nGpuIndexIVFScalarQuantizer and GpuIndexIVFPQ,\nwhich are the most useful for large-scale indexing.\nThe coarse quantizer for the IVF indices can be on\neither CPU or GPU. The GPU index objects have\nthe same interface as their CPU counterparts and\nthe functions index cpu to gpu / index gpu to cpu\nconvert between them.\nMultiple GPUs are also\nsupported. GPU indexes can take inputs and outputs\nin GPU or CPU memory as input and output, and\nPython interface can handle Pytorch tensors.\nAdvanced options for Faiss components and indices\nMany Faiss components expose internal parameters\nto fine-tune the tradeoff between metrics: number of\niterations of k-means, batch sizes for brute-force dis-\ntance computations, etc.\nDefault parameter values\nwere chosen to work reasonably well in most cases.\nMulti-threading\nFaiss relies on OpenMP to handle\nmulti-threading. By default, Faiss switches to multi-\nthreading processing if it is beneficial, for example,\nat training and batch addition time.\nFaiss multi-\nthreading behavior may be controlled with standard\nOpenMP environment variables and functions, such\nas omp set num threads.\nWhen searching a single vector, Faiss does not\nspawn multiple threads.\nHowever, when batched\nqueries are provided, Faiss processes them in par-\nallel, exploiting the effectiveness of the CPU cache\nand batched linear algebra operations. This is faster\nthan calling search from multiple threads. Therefore,\nqueries should be submitted by batches if possible.\n7.4\nInterfacing with external storage\nFaiss indexes are based on simple storage classes,\nmainly std::vector to make copy-construction eas-\nier. The default implementation of IndexIVF is based\non this storage.\nHowever, to give vector database\ndevelopers more control over the storage of inverted\nlists, Faiss provides two lower-level APIs.\nArbitrary inverted lists.\nThe IVF index uses an\nabstract InvertedLists object as its storage.\nThe\nobject exposes routines to read one inverted list,\nadd entries to it and remove entries.\nThe default\nArrayInvertedLists uses in-memory storage.\nAl-\nternatively, OnDiskInvertedLists provides memory-\nmapped storage.\nMore complex implementations can access a key-\nvalue storage either by storing the entire inverted list\nas a value, or by utilizing key prefix scan operations\nlike the one supported by RocksDB to treat multiple\nkeys prefixed by the same identifier as one inverted\nlist. To this end, the InvertedLists implementation\nexposes an InvertedListsIterator and fetches the\ncodes and ids from the underlying key-value store,\nwhich usually exposes a similar iterable interface.\nAdding, updating and removing codes can be dele-\ngated to the underlying key-value store. We provide\nan implementation for RocksDB in rocksdb ivf.\nScanner objects.\nWith the abstraction above, the\nscanning loop is still controlled by Faiss. If the call-\ning code needs to control the looping code, then the\nFaiss IVF index provides an InvertedListScanner\nobject.\nThe scanner’s state includes the current\nquery vector and current inverted list. It provides a\ndistance to code method that, given a code, com-\nputes the distance from the query to the decom-\npressed vector. At a slightly higher level, it loops over\na set of codes and updates a provided result buffer.\nThis abstraction is useful when the inverted lists\nare not stored sequentially or fragmented into sub-\nlists because of metadata filtering [Huang et al., 2020].\nFaiss is used only to perform the coarse quantization\nand the vector encoding.\n8\nFaiss applications\nFaiss is used in many configurations. Hundreds of\nvector search applications rely on it, both within Meta\nand externally. Below we present a few use cases that\nshowcase either an extreme scale or an application\nwith particular impact.\n16\n\n\n=== PDF page 17 ===\n8.1\nTrillion scale index\nFor this example, Faiss is used to index 1.5 trillion vec-\ntors in 144 dimensions. The indexing needs to be ac-\ncurate, therefore the compression of the vectors is lim-\nited to 54 bytes with a PCA to 72 dimensions and 6-bit\nscalar quantizer (PCAR72,SQ6).\nA HNSW coarse quantizer with 10M centroids is\nused for the IndexIVFScalarQuantizer, trained with\na simple distributed GPU k-means (implemented in\nfaiss.clustering).\nAfter the training is finished, the index is built in 3\nphases:\n1. shard over ids: add the input vectors in 2000\nshards independently, producing 2000 indexes\n(that fit in RAM);\n2. shard over lists:\nbuild the 100 indexes corre-\nsponding each to a subset of 100k inverted lists.\nThis is done on 100 different machines, each ac-\ncessing the 2000 sharded indices, and writing the\nresults directly to a shared disk partition;\n3. load the shards: memory-map all 100 indices on\na central machine as 100 OnDiskInvertedLists (a\nmemory map of 83TiB).\nThe central machine that processes queries per-\nforms the coarse quantization and loads the inverted\nlists from the distributed disk partition.\nThe limiting factor is the network bandwidth of the\ncentral machine. Therefore, it is more efficient to dis-\ntribute the search on 20 intermediate servers to spread\nthe load. This brings the search time down to roughly\n1 s per query.\nThis index is built on vanilla Faiss in pure Python,\non commodity CPU servers with hard disk drives and\na regular IP network.\n8.2\nText retrieval\nFaiss is commonly used for natural language process-\ning tasks. In particular, ANNS is relevant for informa-\ntion retrieval [Thakur et al., 2021, Petroni et al., 2021],\nwith applications such as fact checking, entity linking,\nslot filling or open-domain question answering: these\noften rely on retrieving relevant content across a large-\nscale corpus. To that end, embedding models have\nbeen optimized for text retrieval [Izacard et al., 2021,\nLin et al., 2023a].\nFinally,\n[Izacard et al., 2023],\n[Lin et al., 2023b],\n[Shi et al., 2023b] and [Khandelwal et al., 2020] con-\nsist of language models that have been trained to\nintegrate textual retrieval in order to improve their\naccuracy, factuality or compute efficiency.\n8.3\nData mining\nAnother recurrent application of ANNS and Faiss is\nin the mining and curation of large datasets. In par-\nticular, Faiss has been used to mine bilingual texts\nacross very large text datasets retrieved from the web\n[Schwenk and Douze, 2017, Barrault et al., 2023], or to\norganize a language model’s training corpus in order\nto group together series of documents covering simi-\nlar topics [Shi et al., 2023a].\nIn the image domain, [Oquab et al., 2023] leverages\nFaiss to remove duplicates from a dataset containing\n1.3B images.\nIt then relies on efficient indexing in\norder to mine a curated dataset whose distribution\nmatches the distribution of a target dataset.\n8.4\nContent Moderation\nOne of the major applications of Faiss is the de-\ntection and remediation of harmful content at scale.\nHuman labelled examples of policy violating im-\nages and videos are embedded with models such\nas SSCD [Pizzi et al., 2022] and stored in a Faiss in-\ndex. To decide if a new image or video would vio-\nlate some policies, a multi-stage classification pipeline\nfirst embeds the content and searches the Faiss in-\ndex for similar labelled examples, typically utilizing\nrange queries. The results are aggregated and pro-\ncessed through additional machine classification or\nhuman verification. Since the impact of mistakes is\nhigh, good representations should discriminate per-\nceptually similar and different content, and accurate\nsimilarity search is required even at billions to trillion-\nscale.\nThe former problem motivated the Image\nand Video Similarity Challenges [Douze et al., 2021,\nPizzi et al., 2023].\n9\nConclusion\nIn this work, we presented the task of efficient approx-\nimate nearest-neighbor vector search, and exposed\nhow Faiss addresses the most common practitioners’\nproblems in that space. Indeed, Faiss contains a wide\nset of methods that, once combined, achieve varying\ntradeoffs in terms of training time, throughput, mem-\nory usage and accuracy. Most of the use cases and\nexperiments mentioned in this paper are presented\nin more detail and with corresponding code in Faiss’\nwiki pages.\nReferences\n[Amara et al., 2022] Amara, K., Douze, M., Sablay-\nrolles, A., and J´egou, H. (1998).\nA quantitative analysis and perfor-\nmance study for similarity-search methods in high-\ndimensional spaces.\nIn VLDB, volume 98, pages\n194–205.\n21\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 102262]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 102262, "selected_characters": 102262, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-TORCH-NUMERIC/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "bb6b2d8154a10f8f3b15e5ae4465cbc8d833067046964fc6b4d66dc036131b45", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== docs/source/notes/numerical_accuracy.rst =====\n.. _numerical_accuracy:\n\nNumerical accuracy\n==================\n\nIn modern computers, floating point numbers are represented using IEEE 754 standard.\nFor more details on floating point arithmetic and IEEE 754 standard, please see\n`Floating point arithmetic <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floating-point_arithmetic>`_\nIn particular, note that floating point provides limited accuracy (about 7 decimal digits\nfor single precision floating point numbers, about 16 decimal digits for double precision\nfloating point numbers) and that floating point addition and multiplication are not\nassociative, so the order of the operations affects the results.\nBecause of this, PyTorch is not guaranteed\nto produce bitwise identical results for floating point computations that are\nmathematically identical. Similarly, bitwise identical results are not guaranteed across\nPyTorch releases, individual commits, or different platforms. In particular, CPU and GPU\nresults can be different even for bitwise-identical inputs and even after controlling for\nthe sources of randomness.\n\nBatched computations or slice computations\n------------------------------------------\n\nMany operations in PyTorch support batched computation, where the same operation is performed\nfor the elements of the batches of inputs. An example of this is :meth:`torch.mm` and\n:meth:`torch.bmm`. It is possible to implement batched computation as a loop over batch elements,\nand apply the necessary math operations to the individual batch elements, for efficiency reasons\nwe are not doing that, and typically perform computation for the whole batch. The mathematical\nlibraries that we are calling, and PyTorch internal implementations of operations can produce\nslightly different results in this case, compared to non-batched computations. In particular,\nlet ``A`` and ``B`` be 3D tensors with the dimensions suitable for batched matrix multiplication.\nThen ``(A@B)[0]`` (the first element of the batched result) is not guaranteed to be bitwise\nidentical to ``A[0]@B[0]`` (the matrix product of the first elements of the input batches)\neven though mathematically it's an identical computation.\n\nSimilarly, an operation applied to a tensor slice is not guaranteed to produce results that are\nidentical to the slice of the result of the same operation applied to the full tensor. E.g. let\n``A`` be a 2-dimensional tensor. ``A.sum(-1)[0]`` is not guaranteed to be bitwise equal to\n``A[:,0].sum()``.\n\n\nExtremal values\n---------------\n\nWhen inputs contain large values such that intermediate results may overflow the range of the\nused datatype, the end result may overflow too, even though it is representable in the original\ndatatype. E.g.:\n\n.. code:: python\n\n    import torch\n    a=torch.tensor([1e20, 1e20]) # fp32 type by default\n    a.norm() # produces tensor(inf)\n    a.double().norm() # produces tensor(1.4142e+20, dtype=torch.float64), representable in fp32\n\n.. _Linear Algebra Stability:\n\nLinear algebra (``torch.linalg``)\n---------------------------------\n\nNon-finite values\n\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\n\nThe external libraries (backends) that ``torch.linalg`` uses provide no guarantees on their behaviour\nwhen the inputs have non-finite values like ``inf`` or ``NaN``. As such, neither does PyTorch.\nThe operations may return a tensor with non-finite values, or raise an exception, or even segfault.\n\nConsider using :func:`torch.isfinite` before calling these functions to detect this situation.\n\nExtremal values in linalg\n\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\n\nFunctions within ``torch.linalg`` have more `Extremal Values`_ than other PyTorch functions.\n\n:ref:`linalg solvers` and :ref:`linalg inverses` assume that the input matrix ``A`` is invertible. If it is close to\nbeing non-invertible (for example, if it has a very small singular value), then these algorithms may silently return\nincorrect results. These matrices are said to be `ill-conditioned <https://nhigham.com/2020/03/19/what-is-a-condition-number/>`_.\nIf provided with ill-conditioned inputs, the result of these functions they may vary when using the same inputs on different\ndevices or when using different backends via the keyword ``driver``.\n\nSpectral operations like ``svd``, ``eig``, and ``eigh`` may also return incorrect results (and their gradients may be infinite)\nwhen their inputs have singular values that are close to each other. This is because the algorithms used to compute these decompositions\nstruggle to converge for these inputs.\n\nRunning the computation in ``float64`` (as NumPy does by default) often helps, but it does not solve these issues in all cases.\nAnalyzing the spectrum of the inputs via :func:`torch.linalg.svdvals` or their condition number via :func:`torch.linalg.cond`\nmay help to detect these issues.\n\n\nTensorFloat-32(TF32) on Nvidia Ampere (and later) devices\n---------------------------------------------------------\n\nOn Ampere (and later) Nvidia GPUs, PyTorch can use TensorFloat32 (TF32) to speed up mathematically intensive operations, in particular matrix multiplications and convolutions.\nWhen an operation is performed using TF32 tensor cores, only the first 10 bits of the input mantissa are read.\nThis may reduce accuracy and produce surprising results (e.g., multiplying a matrix by the identity matrix may produce results that are different from the input).\nBy default, TF32 tensor cores are disabled for matrix multiplications and enabled for convolutions, although most neural network workloads have the same convergence behavior when using TF32 as they have with fp32.\nWe recommend enabling TF32 tensor cores for matrix multiplications with ``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.fp32_precision = \"tf32\"`` (```torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_tf32 = True`` is going to be deprecated) if your network does not need full float32 precision.\nIf your network needs full float32 precision for both matrix multiplications and convolutions, then TF32 tensor cores can also be disabled for convolutions with ``torch.backends.cudnn.conv.fp32_precision = \"ieee\"`` (``torch.backends.cudnn.allow_tf32 = False`` is going to be deprecated).\n\nFor more information see :ref:`TensorFloat32<tf32_on_ampere>`.\n\nReduced Precision Reduction for FP16 and BF16 GEMMs\n----------------------------------------------------\nHalf-precision GEMM operations are typically done with intermediate accumulations (reduction) in single-precision for numerical accuracy and improved resilience to overflow. For performance, certain GPU architectures, especially more recent ones, allow a few truncations of the intermediate accumulation results to the reduced precision (e.g., half-precision). This change is often benign from the perspective of model convergence, though it may lead to unexpected results (e.g., ``inf`` values when the final result should be be representable in half-precision).\nIf reduced-precision reductions are problematic, they can be turned off with\n``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_fp16_reduced_precision_reduction = False``\n\nA similar flag exists for BF16 GEMM operations and is turned on by default. If BF16\nreduced-precision reductions are problematic, they can be turned off with\n``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_bf16_reduced_precision_reduction = False``\n\nFor more information see :ref:`allow_fp16_reduced_precision_reduction<fp16reducedprecision>` and :ref:`allow_bf16_reduced_precision_reduction<bf16reducedprecision>`\n\nReduced Precision Reduction for FP16 and BF16 in Scaled Dot Product Attention (SDPA)\n------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nA naive SDPA math backend, when using FP16/BF16 inputs, can accumulate significant numerical errors due to the usage of low-precision intermediate buffers. To mitigate this issue, the default behavior now involves upcasting FP16/BF16 inputs to FP32. Computations are performed in FP32/TF32, and the final FP32 results are then downcasted back to FP16/BF16. This will improve numerical accuracy of the final output for the math backend with FP16/BF16 inputs, but increases memory usages and may cause the performance regressions in the math backend as computations shift from FP16/BF16 BMM to FP32/TF32 BMM/Matmul.\n\nFor scenarios where reduced-precision reductions are preferred for speed, they can be enabled with the following setting:\n``torch.backends.cuda.allow_fp16_bf16_reduction_math_sdp(True)``\n\n.. _fp16_on_mi200:\n\nReduced Precision FP16 and BF16 GEMMs and Convolutions on AMD Instinct MI200 devices\n------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nOn AMD Instinct MI200 GPUs, the FP16 and BF16 V_DOT2 and MFMA matrix instructions flush input and output denormal values to zero. FP32 and FP64 MFMA matrix instructions do not flush input and output denormal values to zero. The affected instructions are only used by rocBLAS (GEMM) and MIOpen (convolution) kernels; all other PyTorch operations will not encounter this behavior. All other supported AMD GPUs will not encounter this behavior.\n\nrocBLAS and MIOpen provide alternate implementations for affected FP16 operations. Alternate implementations for BF16 operations are not provided; BF16 numbers have a larger dynamic range than FP16 numbers and are less likely to encounter denormal values. For the FP16 alternate implementations, FP16 input values are cast to an intermediate BF16 value and then cast back to FP16 output after the accumulate FP32 operations. In this way, the input and output types are unchanged.\n\nWhen training using FP16 precision, some models may fail to converge with FP16 denorms flushed to zero. Denormal values more frequently occur in the backward pass of training during gradient calculation. PyTorch by default will use the rocBLAS and MIOpen alternate implementations during the backward pass. The default behavior can be overridden using environment variables, ROCBLAS_INTERNAL_FP16_ALT_IMPL and MIOPEN_DEBUG_CONVOLUTION_ATTRIB_FP16_ALT_IMPL. The behavior of these environment variables is as follows:\n\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n|               | forward   | backward  |\n+===============+===========+===========+\n| Env unset     | original  | alternate |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n| Env set to 1  | alternate | alternate |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n| Env set to 0  | original  | original  |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n\nThe following is the list of operations where rocBLAS may be used:\n\n* torch.addbmm\n* torch.addmm\n* torch.baddbmm\n* torch.bmm\n* torch.mm\n* torch.nn.GRUCell\n* torch.nn.LSTMCell\n* torch.nn.Linear\n* torch.sparse.addmm\n* the following torch._C._ConvBackend implementations:\n\n  * slowNd\n  * slowNd_transposed\n  * slowNd_dilated\n  * slowNd_dilated_transposed\n\nThe following is the list of operations where MIOpen may be used:\n\n* torch.nn.Conv[Transpose]Nd\n* the following torch._C._ConvBackend implementations:\n\n  * ConvBackend::Miopen\n  * ConvBackend::MiopenDepthwise\n  * ConvBackend::MiopenTranspose\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 10998]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in
